@@126251309 <h> Empire , knowledge and erasure : Bombing Iran is bombing memory <p> Empire does not begin with bombs . It begins with stories . Before missiles strike , a narrative must be written to make the target bombable . Colonial narrative , as postcolonial historian Ranajit Guha shows in A Conquest Foretold , transforms conquest into destiny . It prepares the public to accept war as not only inevitable but righteous . <p> It is not simply that Iran was bombed on 13 June 2025 . It is that the idea of Iran and its right to hold memory , to produce knowledge , to exist as a civilisational subject was already rendered illegible . <p> The Israeli assault on Iran was defended in familiar terms -- an imminent nuclear threat , national security , surgical precision . But these are not explanations . They are scripts . Like the invasion of Iraq in 2003 , the attack on Iran was rationalised through an architecture of claims that do not require evidence , only repetition . Intelligence reports denying Iran 's weapons programme were irrelevant . The narrative had @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- Empire writes the future in advance . The British conquest of India was not sealed at the Battle of Plassey in 1757 . That battle was a minor military affair . And yet , in British imperial historiography , Plassey was elevated to a grand foundational moment , the start of British rule in India , the point at which the East India Company became a territorial power . <p> As Guha points out , it was this narrative that was repeated in schoolbooks , official dispatches and parliamentary speeches . It provided the ideological foundation for expanding British rule . While the real event was modest and sordid , the story told about it became grand and civilisational . <p> Guha painstakingly shows that conquest must be justified , not just enacted . He draws from the writings of figures like Robert Orme , an official historian of the East India Company , who openly declared that " the sword is the charter " . This chilling phrase captures the heart of imperial logic -- that might creates right . Violence , if victorious , rewrites itself as @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ legitimacy . <p> What Guha reveals is that empires do not just win battles , they write the laws and the history books . They tell the story in such a way that the destruction appears noble , even necessary . The conquered are not only defeated on the ground ; they are also written out of history . <p> This symbolic process , Guha argues , is what transforms the " instant of aggression " into the logic of rule . The colonial archive did not record India 's conquest merely as fact . It reworked it as a historical necessity , a fulfilment of a moral and civilisational order . This allowed future wars , occupations and annexations to be narrated not as violence , but as destiny . Guha calls this " a conquest foretold " , a fate legitimised before it is ever enforced . <p> This continues today . The West does not simply bomb places . It un-names them . In the mainstream media , Iran is rarely presented as a site of knowledge , history or intellectual contribution . It is a shadow @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ alien , frightening . Colonial narratives render people fungible , bombable and , ultimately , forgettable . They reduce cities to targets , its people to collateral and magnificent civilisational archives to dust . <p> Literary theorist Edward Said showed clearly that the Orient was never just misunderstood . It was constructed . It was imagined as timeless , barbaric , hyper-religious and fundamentally unfit for self-rule . This epistemic violence enabled actual violence . When the drones strike , they do so on the back of a long intellectual history that emptied the East of sovereignty . <p> Said 's Orientalism is a study of how European and American thinkers created a fictional image of the East , a world of despots , harems , fanaticism and mystery . This fantasy was not harmless . It underpinned policy , war and occupation . Said wanted readers to understand that power works not only through tanks and armies , but also through language , maps and books . <p> How we speak about a place , whether on TV or in schools , shapes what we believe can or should @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ irrational and dangerous , bombing it becomes not a horror , but a duty . <p> Orientalism , Said shows , is not an error of perception . It is a system of power . Through universities , literature , policy and media , the West defined the East as the inverse of itself : irrational , feminine , despotic . This representation justified intervention . If the East could not govern itself , then governance must be imposed from without . Once internalised , this logic rendered bombing not only possible but legible as order , as responsibility , as peacekeeping . Violence becomes virtue . <p> Iran has a long and magnificent intellectual history . The Academy of Gundishapur in Khuzestan , Iran , was one of the oldest universities in the world . Founded in the third century CE , it was a centre for multilingual scholarship where Sanskrit medical texts were translated into Middle Persian , Greek logic was systematised and Babylonian astronomy refined . It was here that clinical observation became a method of medical teaching , surgery was formalised and knowledge travelled across linguistic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in 1096 or Cambridge in 1209 . <p> The very model of the Western university , its division into faculties , the logic of disputation , the canon of philosophy and science , was shaped by Iranian and Islamic precedents . The Bayt al-Hikma ( House of Wisdom ) in Abbasid Baghdad , deeply influenced by Persian scholarship , became the template for later European institutions . <p> When Arabic and Persian texts were translated into Latin in Andalusia and Sicily , they were not curiosities , they were the architecture of another world being absorbed . There would be no scholastic tradition without al-Farabi and al-Tusi , no experimental method without al-Razi and no algebra without al-Khwarizmi . <p> These networks extended even further south . Persian texts and Islamic jurisprudence travelled along trans-Saharan trade routes into West Africa . At Sankor ? University in Timbuktu , scholars studied Avicenna and al-Ghazali alongside local astronomers and jurists . Libraries in desert towns preserved manuscripts copied in Persian script . These were not isolated developments . They formed part of an intellectual system that preceded and shaped the European Enlightenment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Timbuktu , Persian and Arabic texts travelled along trade routes , translated , adapted and taught across generations . The commentaries of al-Farabi , al-Tusi and Avicenna informed the very structure of the Western university . Algebra , optics and medicine -- none of these can be understood without Iran . <p> This history was not just ignored by European colonialism . It was actively erased . During colonial rule in India , the British administration removed Persian as a court and scholarly language , replacing it with English and thereby severing centuries of transregional intellectual continuity between South Asia , Central Asia and the Iranian plateau . Persian manuscripts were stripped of context and displayed as exotic artefacts in museums . Its philosophy was recast as mysticism . Its scientific legacy was reduced to footnotes or omitted altogether . <p> Tabriz , struck in the June 2025 attack , is not just a military site . It is the city of Shams al-Din Tabrizi , Rumi 's teacher and a centre of Persian mysticism and learning . It is a node in a centuries-old network of philosophy , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Khurasan to Mali . These were not isolated traditions . They were the foundations of a civilisational archive beyond the borders and imagination of Europe . <p> And yet , in the dominant Western imagination , Iran is nothing but a sinister , irrational enemy of the West . <p> This is not ignorance . It is design . It is an instance of what Guha and Said both expose : the strategic construction of the South as a site of absence . A place whose people are forgettable , whose knowledge is disposable , whose destruction is thinkable . <p> We have seen this before . Iraq 's libraries were looted . Mosul 's university was razed . Gaza 's schools , universities and archives were bombed into nothingness . In each case , what is attacked is not just infrastructure but the right to remember , to dream , to transmit . Epistemic erasure is not collateral damage . It is the method of neocolonial domination . <p> To centre the epistemic is not to look away from the dead . It is to insist that their lives @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the people of Iran are not footnotes to someone else 's future . That they are authors , carriers of meaning and custodians of a world that Empire has tried again and again to silence . <p> This war , like all imperial wars , is not just about sovereignty . It is about the terms of knowledge . Who gets to define history . Who gets to remember . <p> Until we refuse the stories that make us invisible and tell our own stories , we will never escape the imperial forces that deny our full and equal humanity . <p> Vashna Jagarnath is a historian , political risk and diversity , equity , and inclusion consultant , labour expert , pan-African and South Asian political analyst and curriculum specialist . 
@@126251312 <h> Middle East 's masked hegemonies : Tyrants wrestle in the name of salvation <p> What unfolds today in the Middle East is not a conventional clash of nation-states pursuing divergent interests . Rather , it is a confrontation between two competing eschatologies -- each seeking to monopolise meaning , to remake the symbolic and political architecture of the region in its image . Israel and Iran are not true opposites ; they are rival expressions of authoritarian impulses , each armed with its own mythologies , institutions and metaphysical claims . <p> The Zionist project is not simply security-driven nationalism but a colonial modernity that positions itself as the centre of global civilisation while relegating the Arab , the Muslim -- and even the Mizrahi Jew -- to the margins of personhood . Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu does not behave as a statesman in a pluralistic democracy but as a high priest of an exclusionary creed presiding over a militarised state that performs structural violence under the guise of " the only democracy in the Middle East " . <p> The West persists in presenting Israel as a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ liberal dialogue , as if statehood were a card game played fairly in a cosmopolitan club . This is fiction . As Israeli historian Ilan Papp ? reminds us , Israel remains a colonial enterprise , morphing from overt military occupation into a subtler regime of epistemic domination and structural control across historical Palestine . <p> Jewish philosopher Yeshayahu Leibowitz once described Zionism 's mutation into a civil religion -- an idolatry of state and army , converting identity into weaponry . From Syria to Lebanon , Gaza to Tehran , Israeli power extends not as a defensive necessity , but as a project of domination . In this vision , " security " becomes a permanent rationale for violence and " the enemy " a theological constant that justifies endless expansion and subjugation . <p> Yet Israel 's core anxiety towards Iran is not rooted in the Islamic Republic 's " revolutionary " character , but in the fact that Tehran disrupts the liberal-Western narrative and exposes the hypocrisies of post-Sykes-Picot legitimacy . <p> But Iran does not offer a liberationist alternative but rather a mirrored authoritarianism -- a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ non-Shiite constituencies instead of embracing pluralism . <p> Despite internal dissent and mounting international pressure , Iran 's ruling elite -- anchored in the Revolutionary Guard and velayat-e faqih -- has doubled down on its own messianic supremacy , excluding minorities , women and secular voices alike . As Iranian philosopher Abdolkarim Soroush cautioned : " When religion becomes a state , both lose their essence -- faith becomes police and power becomes a fatwa . " <p> Amid this strategic delirium , the real choice is not between an Iranian bomb and Israeli bombardment , but between two worldviews , one rooted in coercion and dogma , the other in historical justice and emancipatory rationalism . What truly unsettles both regimes is the emergence of a third paradigm -- a post-sectarian , post-colonial vision that dissolves the myths both sides depend on to maintain symbolic power . <p> The end of Zionism as an imperial structure does not require Israel 's annihilation , but the dismantling of its exclusionary logic . And the end of Iranian tyranny will not be brought about by assassinations or sanctions , but by @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Revolutionary Guard Corps and returning it to the civic hands of its people . <p> Such a future will not be born from geopolitical brinkmanship or theological absolutism , but from a democratic , secular and decentralised reimagination of statehood -- one that acknowledges cultural and ethnic rights , and ties citizenship to historical redress , rather than tribal belonging . <p> This is not a fantasy . It is the only serious alternative to an endless cycle of apocalypse masquerading as salvation . <p> The " New Middle East " can not be a nuclear pact or a lopsided deal crafted by impervious elites . It must be a vision of dignity , placing the human -- not the hegemon -- at its moral and political centre . It must transcend false binaries -- " resistance " versus " capitulation " and " faith " versus " reason " -- and demand the dismantling of both colonial and clerical despotisms . <p> We stand today not between East and West , Sunni and Shiite , but between two visions of power -- one that serves itself through myth and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that speaks for the people , in the name of justice , freedom and truth . <p> Dr Waleed A Madibo is a Fulbright scholar , as well as the founder and president of Sudan Policy Forum . 
@@126251315 <h> Holding the powerful to account : War crimes and the new global reckoning <p> The world is on the brink of war again . War crimes and massacres , genocides are coming to characterise our modern world . " These words predicted the scale of atrocity during the Nazi invasion of Europe . <p> Benjamin believed that barbarism was being transmitted from one hand to another . Almost a century before him , American philosopher Ralph Waldo Emerson raised a very similar concern about the trajectory of civilisation , remarking , " The course of civilisation is an endless train of felonies " and it seems this train is still on the move . <p> The 20th century was full of genocides and crimes against humanity . Colonial atrocities , genocides and mass massacres , along with two world wars , seemed to establish a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Crawshaw 's recent book , Prosecuting the Powerful : War Crimes and the Battle for Justice , is an urgent call for justice . As an award-winning journalist and historian , his book is a document of crises in the struggle against war crimes . <p> Reports , court documents and registers are seamlessly woven into his acute sense of justice and hope . This results from his experiences and what he witnessed over two decades on the front lines of justice through his work at Human Rights Watch , Amnesty International and Freedom from Torture . <p> His commitment to justice is clearly seen in his covering " stories of human rights abuses in the hope of making change " . These observations from the field add to his judgments throughout the book . <p> The book provides us with a broad perspective on the battle against injustice , registering the failures and successes over a century . <p> For Crawshaw , impunity for war crimes is no longer a given . Now , he says , " the slogan ' Send him to The Hague ! ' is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ held accountable " . <p> The International Criminal Court 's ( ICC ) indictments of Russian leader Vladimir Putin and Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu endorse this hope of bringing the powerful to justice . <p> The struggle for justice is not always fulfilled . As he notes , " We have seen genocide convictions in international and domestic courts for everything from Rwanda and Bosnia to the rape and killings of thousands of Yazidis in Iraq and Syria by Islamic State . " <p> World history abounds with silences about war crimes . It took former European colonial powers Germany , Britain and France the best part of a century to admit their crimes against humanity in Africa . <p> There are many killings still shrouded in silence in the Middle East , which are seldom discussed in these terms . However , governments and state leaders always react to genocide or massacre convictions . <p> Most of the discussions around justice , law and crime in the book are based on Polish lawyer and staunch advocate of human rights Raphael Lemkin 's arguments . His ideas seem to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the one who defined the term " genocide " as " a coordinated plan of different actions aiming at the destruction of essential foundations of the life of national groups , with the aim of annihilating the groups themselves " . <p> For instance , he says , " Lemkin would describe Moscow 's treatment of Ukraine as ' perhaps the classic example of Soviet genocide ... the destruction of the Ukrainian nation ' " . <p> In the fight against war crimes , the Nuremberg trials are cited as a driving force to bring justice to criminals . Crawshaw maintains : " Nuremberg helped create a new language around human rights and would become a shorthand for those seeking accountability in conflict zones around the world . " <p> Lemkin , who was an advisor to the Nuremberg court , is quoted as saying , ' The Allies decided their case against a past Hitler but refused to envisage future Hitlers . They did not want to , or could not , establish a rule of international law that would prevent and punish future crimes of the same type @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ law in Nuremberg and the rebirth of humanity to which we all belong " . <p> Later , the establishment of the Yugoslavian and Rwandan tribunals was significant . These two war crimes courts which " prosecuted genocide meant the practical significance of Lemkin 's legacy could be seen for the first time " . <p> Prosecuting war crimes should become more common for peace and the future of humanity . The establishment of the ICC is considered as the continuation and universalising of the Nuremberg Trials . <p> Some African governments ' acknowledgments of the ICC are cited in the book as exemplary : " There were productive collaborations between African governments and The Hague . The court 's first investigation came in response to a request from the government of the Democratic Republic of Congo , in the context of the conflict in eastern Congo in which millions had died . " <p> Another outstanding move mentioned in the book is the Senegalese government and the African Union jointly creating the Extraordinary African Chambers in the Courts of Senegal , which prosecuted crimes committed in Chad under Hiss @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Africa 's bid to bring justice to Gaza is also cited as a remarkable move . <p> What Prosecuting the Powerful shows is that all challenges against warlords and crimes against humanity come from individual moves , citing exemplary figures such as ICC chief prosecutor Karim Khan . <p> Despite all obstacles , justice will prevail ! <p> Prosecuting the powerful : War Crimes and the Battle for Justice , is published by The Bridge Street Press .

@@126292369 <p> As Israel and the US battle Iran in the Middle East , the ripple effects are likely to be felt in Africa . <p> For more than four decades Iran has served as a central engine of Islamist terrorism , using its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps and a network of proxy militias to destabilise the region . From its backing of Hezbollah in Lebanon to funding Shi'a militias in Iraq and its unwavering support for Hamas and Palestinian Islamic Jihad in Gaza , the Iranian regime has perfected a model of asymmetrical warfare . <p> This model relies on ideological radicalism , proxy forces and complex terror financing networks that has plunged the region into repeated cycles of violence . Unfortunately , this influence is not confined to the Middle East and also affects Africa . <p> As in the Middle East , Iran supports military operations in various African countries -- most notably in Sudan and with the Polisario Front in Western Sahara . But Tehran 's strategy extends beyond hard power . Through a broad soft-power campaign , it aims to entrench its ideological , religious and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ this influence is highly visible . Since the 1980s Iran has backed the Islamic Movement of Nigeria , a Shi'a group modelled in some respects on Hezbollah . Before the 1979 Iranian Revolution , Shi'a Islam had little to no footprint in Nigeria . Today it is estimated that 2-million to 3-million Nigerians identify as Shi'a , a transformation driven by Iranian-supported institutions that continue to offer ideological training , financial backing and organisational support . <p> In parallel with its religious outreach , Iran has accelerated its economic and political engagement with Africa . In 2023 it launched eight business centres across the continent and hosted a series of economic summits in Tehran aimed at cementing Africa 's role as a strategic trade partner . These economic overtures are often linked to political and military incentives : Iran has offered combat drones to African governments in exchange for access to uranium , and has sought basing rights in countries such as Sudan in return for arms shipments . <p> At the same time Tehran 's propaganda machine has expanded . Hausa TV , a state-sponsored channel broadcasting in one @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pro-Iran and anti-Israel messaging to a regional audience of over 50-million people . These media operations serve as a critical arm of Iran 's information warfare in Africa . <p> A child 's toy lies amid debris at a building where four people were killed by an Iranian missile strike on June 24 in Beersheba . Its tactics , organisational models and financial structures are being emulated by extremist groups across the continent -- with devastating consequences . <p> According to the US Africa Command , while only 4% of global Islamic jihadists were based in Africa in 2008 , that figure has since risen to 40% . According to the Africa Centre for Strategic Studies , the number of violent incidents linked to Islamist groups in Africa nearly quadrupled between 2014 and 2023 -- from 1,812 to 6,756 attacks . <p> A recent study by the Middle East Africa Research Institute notes that while terrorism was once concentrated in North Africa , it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ countries such as Nigeria , Somalia , Mozambique and Democratic Republic of Congo . <p> One of the most horrific manifestations of this violence occurred recently in Yelewata , Benue State , Nigeria , where militants massacred up to 200 Christians on the night of June 14 this year . The attackers targeted already-displaced families , setting their shelters on fire while they slept and hacking to death those who tried to flee , according to NGO Aid to the Church in Need . <p> " What I saw was truly gruesome . People were slaughtered . Corpses were scattered everywhere , " said local priest Ukuma Jonathan Angbianbee . At his Sunday Angelus address , Pope Leo XIV condemned the attack , calling it a " terrible massacre " and offering prayers for the internally displaced people who were targeted . The Pope highlighted the vulnerability of rural Christian communities in Nigeria who have become frequent victims of Islamist violence . <p> Islamic State-linked networks have been known to launder money through SA financial channels and redirect it to support extremist activities across East Africa . <p> This atrocity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ pattern of jihadist brutality that includes beheadings , suicide bombings , child soldier recruitment , church burnings and the abduction of women and girls for forced marriage . The tactics closely mirror those used by the Islamic State in Iraq and Syria , and by Hamas in its attacks on Israeli civilians . The spread of this model across Africa highlights the urgency of breaking the supply chains -- ideological , financial and strategic -- that enable it . <p> SA plays a particularly troubling role in this dynamic . Its advanced financial infrastructure , combined with lax oversight , has made it a hub for terror financing . Islamic State-linked networks have been known to launder money through SA financial channels and redirect it to support extremist activities across East Africa . <p> In 2023 SA was placed on the Financial Action Task Force greylist , signalling serious deficiencies in its monitoring of illicit financial flows . Compounding the problem are the country 's political and commercial ties to the Islamic Republic of Iran . A weakened Iran could mean the collapse of its influence networks on the continent , including proxy groups , ideological institutions and terror financing systems . <p> For many African communities that have suffered under the shadow of Islamist violence inspired or supported by Iran , this could offer a long-overdue chance to restore peace , security and self-determination . <p> * Shulman is executive director of the Middle East Africa Research Institute .

@@126292641 <h> Coal and South Africa 's complicity in the genocide in Gaza <p> Exporting coal to Israel contradicts South Africa 's stance on that country 's genocide in Gaza . <p> South Africa has rightly earned praise across the world for taking a principled stance against Israel 's genocidal assault on Gaza . But we can not claim the mantle of global moral leadership while continuing to do business as usual with an apartheid regime now engaged in open genocide . Our ongoing export of coal to Israel -- an arrangement that materially supports the very war effort we have condemned at The Hague -- is a glaring contradiction in our otherwise principled positions . <p> According to two new briefings -- one from the Palestinian Youth Movement and another from researchers working to align our foreign policy with our public commitments -- South African coal continues to fuel Israel 's energy grid , including its military installations , surveillance infrastructure and illegal settlements in the occupied West Bank . Worse still , our role has grown more central since Colombia , formerly Israel 's largest supplier , issued a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ state . <p> Colombia has honoured contracts signed before its decree , but it has committed to ending future shipments . South Africa , by contrast , has yet to take any meaningful action . A number of corporates continue to sell South African coal to Israel . Glencore , a company globally notorious for unethical conduct , is the dominant player but others , such as African Rainbow Minerals are also complicit . <p> This raises an urgent question : How can a country that seeks to halt genocide at the International Court of Justice ( ICJ ) be one of the top energy suppliers to the regime perpetrating that very genocide ? <p> The ICJ case , supported by a growing number of Global South countries , has shifted the diplomatic terrain . The South African government has also co-chaired The Hague Group , a new alliance of countries seeking to defend international law against systematic violations in Gaza . <p> These steps matter . But our ports -- especially Richards Bay -- have remained open to the same coal shipments that sustain Israel 's military occupation . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 7 October 2023 , at least 11 coal shipments from South Africa have arrived in Israel , according to vessel-tracking data . These shipments total more than 1.4 million tonnes -- about 25% of Israel 's coal imports during this period . <p> The majority of these shipments were loaded at Richards Bay and docked at the ports of Hadera and Ashkelon -- home to the Orot Rabin and Rutenberg coal-fired power stations . These plants provide about 17.5% of Israel 's electricity . Coal has a central role in sustaining both civilian infrastructure and military operations in Israel . <p> Israel does not produce its own coal . It is entirely dependent on imports to run its coal-fired plants and is , therefore , vulnerable to coordinated boycotts . <p> The Stichting Onderzoek Multinationale Ondernemingen -- Centre for Research on Multinational Corporations -- has shown that when states supply energy that directly supports illegal occupation or genocide , they are in violation of international law . It explicitly recommends that states " end the supply of coal to Israel where there is no means of ensuring it does not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have no such guarantees . And even if we did , they would be impossible to verify . The only ethical and lawful option is to end coal exports entirely . <p> If South Africa is to retain credibility in its international legal claims , its domestic trade and industrial policy must be consistent with those claims . This is not simply a question of ethics ; it is a question of coherence in governance . A state that seeks provisional measures at the ICJ to halt genocide can not continue to facilitate that same genocide through commodity flows . This contradiction not only undermines South Africa 's legal arguments , it also weakens the ability of Global South countries to use international law as a terrain of struggle . <p> There is no neutral trade in the context of genocide . Every shipment of coal exported to Israel either contributes to or withholds material support from a war machine . If we claim to stand with the victims , our policies must withdraw complicity from the perpetrators . <p> Of course , any conversation about South Africa 's trade @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ our economic crisis . With nearly half the population unemployed and millions pushed into crushing poverty , the social cost of job losses is devastating . For working-class people and their families , even small disruptions to income can be catastrophic . The crisis of mass unemployment -- a structural failure rooted in both apartheid and post-apartheid economic mismanagement -- hangs over every policy decision . <p> This is why it 's important to approach this issue with care and clarity . Before acting , we need to establish , with precision , whether a coal boycott would lead to any actual job losses . Academic and NGO researchers should work alongside government and trade unions to produce a shared and verified understanding of the scale and scope of South Africa 's coal exports to Israel -- and what risks , if any , exist for workers should those exports be halted . <p> If there is a real risk to jobs , the next step must be to find alternative markets . This is not an impossible task . South Africa 's coal sector is vast and the volume @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 1.2% of coal exports and just 0.02% of GDP . Redirecting those shipments is a logistical challenge , not an economic impossibility . If there is political will , viable alternatives can be found . <p> This is not about asking workers to carry the cost of a moral position . It 's about building the political and practical basis to ensure that our country can act on its principles without deepening the hardship of the poor . The trade union movement has already made this clear . <p> In a powerful statement issued in August 2024 , trade union federation Cosatu declared its support for the call on Glencore to stop sending coal to Israel , stating that " fuelling apartheid and genocide is a crime " . But the federation also rightly called on the government to ensure that a boycott protects jobs . <p> Trade policy in a democratic state is not the exclusive domain of multinational firms or economic ministries . It must be subordinate to the constitutional obligations of the state and to its commitments under international law . South Africa 's Constitution affirms the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ affirms the urgency of halting genocide . The question now is whether its trade policy will follow suit . <p> Dr Imraan Buccus is senior research associate at ASRI and a research fellow at the University of the Free State . 
@@126292737 <h> Key topics <p> Iran 's military gutted ; future now hinges on internal uprising or reform <p> Sign up for your early morning brew of the BizNews Insider to keep you up to speed with the content that matters . Register here . <p> If you prefer WhatsApp for updates , sign up to the BizNews channel here . <p> By Ivo Vegter <p> Iran is no longer a major Middle-Eastern power . Its entire strategy lies in ruins , and its allies are largely defeated . <p> As the sun set on the bloody slaughter of 7 October 2023 , it was hard to imagine what the future would hold . <p> That Israel would respond with force against Hamas was a certainty . That Israel would be blamed by its enemies for doing so was also entirely predictable . <p> That the invasion by Hamas @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Resistance " in little more than a year and a half , however , few would have predicted . <p> And yet , that 's what happened . Iran no longer controls its own airspace . Its military has been gutted by decapitation strikes against senior ranks . Its missile manufacturing capability has been severely curtailed , and its nuclear facilities heavily damaged . Its feared Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps is a paper tiger . Its carefully nurtured non-state allies have gone silent . <p> Axis of Resistance <p> The brutal , undemocratic , and repressive Shi'ite theocracy of Iran , which had been the malevolent heart of what George W. Bush once called the " Axis of Evil " -- referring to Iraq , Iran and North Korea -- responded by establishing what it called an " Axis of Resistance " against Israel and the United States . <p> Its religious leaders described these countries as " Little Satan " and " Great Satan " , and repeatedly vowed to destroy Israel and push back against American influence in the Middle East . <p> It maintained its end @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ recruiting Bashar al-Assad 's Syrian government and a rogue 's gallery of non-state armed extremist groups to form its Axis of Resistance . <p> These organisations included Hezbollah in Lebanon , which was once considered the most powerful non-state actor in the world ; Hamas , a Sunni group which tyrannised the Gaza Strip ; Yemen 's Houthi militias ; the Syrian Social Nationalist Party , which was allied to Assad 's ruling Arab Socialist Ba'ath Party ; member groups of the Palestine Liberation Organisation , such as the Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine -- General Command , and the Democratic Front for the Liberation of Palestine ; Islamic Resistance in Iraq , and the Popular Mobilisation Forces , also in Iraq ; and Shi-ite militias based in Bahrain . <p> A motley crew with sometimes divergent interests and ideologies , most of these organisations are recognised internationally as terrorist groups , and all share the explicit objective of wiping Israel off the map and pushing the Jews into the sea . <p> As a consequence , Iran is widely viewed as the leading state sponsor of terrorism @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Resistance as a defensive shield that would protect it ( and its nascent nuclear weapons programme ) from direct Israeli or American attack . The knowledge that an Israeli strike on Iran would precipitate attacks against Israeli military and civilian targets from all directions , was thought to be a sufficient deterrent . <p> Conversely , the Axis of Resistance was viewed as a " ring of fire " around Israel . It " aimed to annihilate and wipe out Israel from existence , " according to Abdul Majeed Awad , head of Hamas 's political and media relations office in Lebanon , shortly after the 7 October attack . <p> Instead , Israel wiped the Axis of Resistance from existence . Its most prominent members lie prostrate and powerless while Israel overflew their air space to dismantle their patron 's military capability with pin-point accuracy . <p> Lit the fuse <p> The Hamas operation on 7 October 2023 that lit the fuse was supported by Iran after the fact , but also took it by surprise . <p> Hamas was never entirely under Iran 's control , did not @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its own . Though they amounted to the same thing -- the destruction of Israel -- Hamas acted in a way that Iran would not have approved , had it been asked for permission . <p> It would have known that attacking Israel while it was distracted by its own internal conflicts -- involving a hardline prime minister facing corruption charges and widespread popular protests -- would likely galvanise the Israelis , strengthen its prime minister 's position , and give it the excuse it needed to move against the Axis of Resistance . <p> This , Israel did . It started with a campaign against Hamas that many thought unnecessarily brutal , but which Israel vowed to sustain until the last of the hostages ( or their remains ) were released , and Hamas was disarmed , never to rule Gaza again . <p> Demonstrating the reach of its intelligence bureau , Mossad , thousands of pagers used by Hezbollah exploded on 17 September 2024 . A day later , hundreds of walkie-talkies did the same . A reported 1,500 enemy fighters , many of them senior leaders , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ air strikes , and targeted attacks against newly appointed leadership figures , Israel critically weakened Hezbollah , to the point where it posed little further threat . <p> In Syria , rebel fighters took advantage of Israel 's war against Hezbollah to mount a lightning offensive against the Ba'athist government of Bashar al-Assad . Within days , the Assad regime fell and Assad and his family fled to asylum in Russia . <p> A Sunni militant group , Hay'at Tahrir al-Sham , proclaimed victory , and its leader Ahmed al-Sharaa , became the de facto ruler of Syria , later becoming president of a transitional government . He has stated that he wants Syria to become a " normal country " , has promised religious tolerance for non-Muslims , and swore off Islamic extremism and terrorism . <p> Israel , having waited until the fall of Damascus to exploit the power vacuum , swept in to destroy Syria 's military capabilities , both on land and at sea , and including its chemical weapons facilities . <p> With that , the only state member of the Axis of Resistance , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ golden ring of the resistance chain in the region " , was gone . <p> The Houthis , another Iranian proxy which controls large swathes of Yemen and opposes the internationally recognised government of Yemen , joined the Israel-Hamas war on 19 October 2023 . <p> Demanding an end to Israel 's action against Hamas , it ineffectually lobbed missiles at Israel 's " Iron Dome " defence system , and more effectively fired missiles at international merchant shipping passing through the Gulf of Aden into the Red Sea on the way to ( or from ) the Suez Canal . <p> These attacks deterred shipping through the strait between Yemen and Djibouti , and effectively shut down the major Israeli trade port of Eilat . <p> Israeli , American , and British retaliation has significantly degraded Houthi capabilities , but have not dissuaded them from further attacks . While the group continues to pose a threat to civilian shipping and global trade , it does not pose an existential threat to Israel . <p> Like Hamas and Hezbollah , the Houthis have been entirely silent while Israeli air strikes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ A day after the International Atomic Energy Agency ( IAEA ) declared Iran to be in violation of its nuclear obligations under international agreements , on 12 June 2025 , even though renewed negotiations between the United States and Iran were still being mooted , Israel struck . It was n't the first time Israel had attacked Iran directly since the war in Gaza broke out , but it was the first comprehensive campaign designed to curtail its nuclear weapons ambitions and military capabilities . <p> Once again , the long arm of Mossad helped . Secret agents had established a covert drone base near Tehran , from where Iran 's air defences and missile infrastructure were sabotaged , giving Israel complete air superiority . Israel also had locations for many senior officials and military targets , and agents inside Iran to assassinate military leadership . <p> Iran neutered <p> In the 12 days since Israel launched its campaign against Iran , it has destroyed a majority of Iran 's air defences and missile launch sites , as well as missile manufacturing plants . It has struck military and other @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ corridor between Israel and Iran and Iranian airspace itself . <p> The US , for its part , used B2 long-range heavy bombers to drop 14 of its largest " bunker buster " bombs on Iran 's three most heavily fortified nuclear facilities , where Iran was clearly continuing its pursuit of nuclear weapons . <p> It is unclear to what extent these facilities were damaged or destroyed , and Iran appears to have moved its stock of highly enriched uranium -- enough to make several bombs -- to an undisclosed location . The IAEA has lost track of this material . <p> Iran has lobbed a few missiles at Israel . The vast majority were intercepted , either by Israel , or by allied countries including the US , France , the UK , Saudi Arabia , and Jordan . A handful got through , causing some damage in Tel Aviv and killing several dozen civilians . Iran has also attacked an American base in Qatar , reportedly without causing any casualties . <p> At the time of writing , a tense cease-fire appears to be taking hold . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ appears to be dead in the water . It is incapable of mounting any substantial military response to Israeli and American strikes . Its allies in the Axis of Resistance have been notable only for their complete absence from the war . <p> Iran has lost . <p> Israel 's future <p> What is clear is that the Axis of Resistance has been dismantled . The future of the region , however , is not yet apparent . <p> While Israel will continue to act against terrorism and remnants of the Axis of Resistance , it now clearly has the upper hand in the Middle East . It is now free to continue the normalisation of relations with the Sunni Arab states that surround it . <p> This process began slowly , with the Israel-Egypt peace treaty in 1979 and the Israel -- Jordan peace treaty in 1994 but accelerated with the Abraham Accords in 2020 involving the United Arab Emirates , Bahrain , Sudan , and Morocco . <p> Unofficially , relations have been warming , and cooperation and trade increasing , between Israel and Sunni Arab nations for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ clear is how the Palestinian conflict will now be resolved . Israel will be in a stronger position vis- ? -vis the armed militias ranged against it , and those militias will likely enjoy far less support from Iran in future . <p> A two-state solution might still be on the cards , but it is no longer the only plausible outcome . Meanwhile , Israel 's prime minister will have to face the music eventually , which could significantly soften the country 's approach towards Palestine . <p> Iran 's future <p> In Iran , the future is even more uncertain . Neither the US nor Israel appear to have the appetite to go any further and potentially effect " regime change " . Having largely disarmed Iran , they will leave the Iranian people to determine their own future . <p> Over the decades since 1979 , there have been many popular uprisings against the theocratic extremists that have a stranglehold on the country . The time seems ripe for a new uprising aimed at overthrowing the brutal , ultra-conservative mullahs . <p> Both Israel and the US @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to support the restoration of the Iranian monarchy as an alternative to religious rule . <p> That would be a mistake , as it was back in 1953 , when they engineered a coup in favour of Shah Mohammed Reza Pahlavi only to produce a corrupt authoritarian regime that was overthrown by popular revolt in 1979 . <p> That revolution began as a pluralistic democratic movement , before being hijacked by the Shi'ite extremists that subjected the Iranian parliament to the ultimate authority of the supreme religious leader . <p> Although it does n't appear so on the surface , secular liberal democracy has a long and venerable history in Iran , and many brave and vocal supporters among the Iranian people . It will be up to those people to rise up against their failed authoritarian rulers , and demand that power , and freedom , be surrendered to the people . <p> A new flowering of freedom in Iran could be the final nail in the coffin of state support for religious extremism and terrorism in the Middle East . <p> *This article was first published by Daily @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @

@@126340763 <h> How will the Iran crisis impact Africa ? <p> Israel , Iran and the United States ' ( US ) escalating hostilities have sent geopolitical tremors far beyond the Middle East . With the fragile ceasefire holding for now , the world waits tensely to see if broader regional or global conflict will erupt . <p> The first danger lies in the security sphere . The conflict risks drawing multiple global powers -- Russia , China , North Atlantic Treaty Organization ( NATO ) -- into yet another Middle Eastern quagmire . Historically , when the great powers collide , Africa often becomes an unintended theatre of competition and collateral . <p> The scars of the Ukraine and Gaza conflicts are still apparent in the myriad economic , diplomatic and security shocks . There is a worrying sense of d ? j ? vu in the current situation . <p> If global tensions spill into new ideological or military standoffs , African countries could get trapped in the messy middle . North and East African states face particular exposure to direct risks from primary and proxy actors . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Israel 's assets in Eritrea , could become the focus of retaliatory strikes by Iran or its regional proxies , such as the Houthis . <p> There is also an indirect security risk arising from a reprioritisation of Western strategic interests . With defence budgets and attention stretched by the Ukraine war and domestic concerns , Africa 's security sectors may find themselves increasingly under-resourced and unable to manage transnational threats . <p> Testament to Africa 's declining geostrategic value in Western security psyche , the continent received little attention during recent G7 and NATO meetings . Nigeria , Cameroon , Chad , Mali , Burkina Faso and Niger are already feeling the brunt of Western retrenchment in the Sahelian theatre . Further disengagement would leave them even more vulnerable to insurgencies . <p> Human security -- often overlooked -- is also at stake . Food insecurity , climate shocks and governance deficits already strain many African states . Fresh pressures could arise if Middle Eastern instability fuels large-scale migration or refugee flows into Europe . European diversion of development funding to manage such spillovers could further undermine safety @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and sectarian tensions , exacerbated by Middle East developments , may also inflame local disputes in Africa 's multi-faith societies . A concern is Nigeria where Shiite groups could engage in attacks in solidarity with Iran . <p> Compounding these risks is the spectre of nuclear proliferation . If Iran abandons nuclear restraint , other regional players may follow , raising chances of an arms race with global consequences . <p> For Africa -- a strong proponent of nuclear disarmament under the Pelindaba Treaty -- such a shift would weaken its influence in future global arms control negotiations . <p> Diplomatically , pressure on African states to take sides will intensify . They may be forced into uncomfortable choices -- between Washington and Tehran , or the US and its rivals in Moscow or Beijing -- with sanctions or aid withdrawal the price for defiance . <p> South Africa is in a particularly delicate position . Its inconsistent foreign policy has left it walking a narrow tightrope . Washington has sparred with Pretoria over its stance on Israel , its domestic policies , proximity to Iran , and its alignment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sign of sympathy towards Iran . <p> Multilateral institutions , especially the United Nations Security Council and expanded BRICS+ , will also be tested . Iran 's recent accession to BRICS has added complexity and may strain the bloc 's unity . African members like South Africa , Egypt and Ethiopia could face awkward choices should the bloc lean towards favouring Tehran . <p> The implications are profound . A world that abandons international law for raw power threatens the very framework that protects smaller , less powerful states . For Africa , this is an existential issue -- without rules-based multilateralism , the continent 's collective influence diminishes . <p> Worse , a global slide into ' might is right ' politics may embolden expansionist African leaders . Ethiopia 's Abiy Ahmed Ali , Rwanda 's Paul Kagame and Morocco 's King Mohammed VI could feel encouraged to pursue territorial ambitions under the cover of global disorder . Others may take ' preventive ' strikes against perceived enemies like the US and Israel -- illegal under current international law . <p> Africa 's collective voting strength at global forums @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ weakens , so too does Africa 's leverage on crucial matters such as debt restructuring , climate finance and development aid . Only through a unified , strategic voice can the continent avoid marginalisation . <p> Yet the most immediate and tangible impact is economic . Global uncertainty traditionally sparks a ' flight to safety ' , strengthening the US dollar and weakening African currencies . This raises debt servicing costs for African sovereigns , many of which are heavily dollar-indebted . Bond yields could spike , and fiscal pressures -- already strained by post-pandemic recovery and fallout from the Ukraine war -- would tighten further . <p> Energy security is another looming flashpoint . Disruption of the Strait of Hormuz -- conduit for a third of the world 's oil -- would send energy prices soaring . Net oil importers , including many African economies , would suffer painful imported inflation , raising transport , food and energy costs . This would intensify cost-of-living crises and complicate monetary policy just as central banks prepare to loosen rates to spur growth . <p> Broader trade disruptions , coupled with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ global economy into recession . For Africa , the fallout could be severe . Depreciation-driven debt deterioration could complicate restructuring in Zambia , Ethiopia and Ghana . Others -- like Kenya and Nigeria , where debt servicing swallows over 30% of revenue -- would face even tighter fiscal constraints . <p> Smaller economies reliant on customs-sharing arrangements , like Lesotho and Eswatini , are also vulnerable , with Southern African Customs Union earnings there already projected to decline by as much as 20% this year . <p> Limited fiscal buffers would force many African governments to divert funds from development spending to cover external gaps -- risking social unrest , political instability and the erosion of already thin reserves . <p> In this volatile context , African leaders must act . Security capacities and collaboration must be bolstered to reduce dependence on foreign powers and cushion against internal and external threats . <p> Diplomacy must become more unified and strategic -- both to protect Africa 's interests and uphold the international rules that safeguard its sovereignty . Economic resilience , through fiscal prudence , dollar de-risking and deeper regional market @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rage abroad , but its sparks are falling on African soil -- and the continent must brace for impact .

@@126389899 <h> OPINION <h> A rare opportunity for SA -- and Israel <p> William Saunderson-Meyer <p> 27 June 2025 <p> William Saunderson-Meyer on the foreign policy benefits of the neutralisation of the Iranian threat <p> JAUNDICED EYE <p> The Israel-Iran war lasted only 12 days but its consequences will shape global politics for years . One positive is that the battering taken by our ally Iran -- following closely on the heels of the collapse of Syria -- could paradoxically greatly benefit South Africa , if President Cyril Ramaphosa was courageous enough to seize the opportunity . <p> The biggest impediment to a good relationship with the United States has been SA 's support of Iran , a nation whose antipathy towards the US ( the ' Great Satan ' ) and Israel ( the ' Little Satan ' ) , drives virtually everything that this repressive theocracy does . While it has been difficult for Iran to challenge directly the military and economic might of the US , Israel has been acutely vulnerable to its near neighbour Iran 's decades-long determination to destroy it . <p> Iran takes a ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ statehood , rejecting Israel 's right to exist and plotting the obliteration of the Jewish state . As Iran 's Supreme Leader Ayatollah Ali Khamenei puts it unashamedly , Israel is a ' malignant cancerous tumour that has to be removed and will be removed ' . Hence the oil-rich regime 's hell-or-high-water pursuit of a ' peaceful ' nuclear programme , which SA has defended at every turn . <p> Iran was also the primary supplier of money and arms to proxy terrorist groups operating against Israel . Hezbollah in Lebanon could not survive without Iran , nor could Palestinian Islamic Jihad ( PIJ ) in Gaza and the West Bank . While Hamas conceivably could continue to operate without Iranian backing in Gaza , it would be as a much weakened political entity and its military threat to Israel would be negligible . <p> It is hardly surprising , then , that SA 's increasing alignment with Iran 's ' axis of resistance ' , as well as its self-appointed role as leading spokesperson for the Global South in siding with Russia and China against the Western democracies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ past dozen or so years . This came to a head in February under the combative presidency of Donald Trump , who issued his punishing executive order against SA , largely because of our ' egregious actions ' in taking ' aggressive positions towards the United States and its allies ' and ' reinvigorating its relations with Iran to develop commercial , military and nuclear arrangements ' . <p> --&gt; <p> The executive order , which summarily terminated trade concessions and aid , will deliver a hard blow -- though ultimately recoverable -- to what remains of our manufacturing sector . More seriously , it posed a potentially fatal threat to our knowledge economy . While trade partners can eventually be replaced , nobody is clamouring to step up and gift billions to support the medical and scientific research that has kept SA 's better universities internationally competitive and , in the field of infectious diseases , globally pre-eminent . <p> Despite these substantial economic costs and President Cyril Ramaphosa speaking fervently about the need to ' reset ' ties with the US -- even claiming , unconvincingly , that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Trump -- the ANC-led unity government has been paralysed with inaction . No new ambassador to Washington has been appointed to replace Ebrahim Rasool , who was declared persona non grata in March . Nearly three months after his appointment as Ramaphosa 's special envoy , Mcebisi Jonas -- chairman of MTN Group that faces US litigation over alleged sanctions violations and support for terrorist organisations through its operations in Iran -- has yet to set foot in America . <p> There are several explanations for this unwillingness or inability to act . However , it boils down to the fact that foreign policy is not determined by pragmatism and national interest , but by ideology and party interest . The foundation of SA 's post-1994 risky friendship with Iran stems from an anti-Western ideological outlook that the ANC has historically shared . This antipathy has been amplified by a powerful Islamist clique within the Department of International Relations and Cooperation that is strong enough within ANC party structures to dictate a far harder line on Middle East matters than some in the Cabinet would like . <p> At least @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is no direct evidence , it is widely believed in Western circles that Iran has at times surreptitiously funded the ANC -- much in the same way as Libyan leader Muammar Gaddafi bought SA 's diplomatic support with large secret donations to the party and its leaders , as former ANC treasurer-general Mathews Phosa documents in his recent memoir . Such political opportunism by a corrupt ANC also has other potential Iranian outlets : access to discounted oil , arms cooperation , and the expansion of nuclear capacity at Koeberg if , in the words of Mineral Affairs and Energy Minister Gwede Mantashe , they ' make the best offer ' . <p> --&gt; <p> The drubbing that Iran has taken in the 12-day war changes everything . Iran 's new precariousness as a functional state , its faltering status as a revolutionary beacon , and its more circumscribed financial circumstances reduce its usefulness to the ANC on all fronts . While institutional habits and ideological inertia may persist , the rational incentives underpinning the relationship have eroded . <p> Whether its nuclear programme has been destroyed , as Trump @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ critics claim , Iran undoubtedly will have reduced capacity for mischief-making in the Middle East while preoccupied with internal reconstruction . Infrastructure has to be rebuilt , lost nuclear and military expertise replaced , and weaponry stocks replenished , all while fretting whether the Ayatollah 's government might be toppled in a popular uprising . <p> As importantly , Iran 's vulnerabilities have been exposed , and its confidence has been shaken . Israel effortlessly thwarted Iran 's much-vaunted missile barrages , while the US demonstrated decisively that it would not sit idle while Tehran lashed out . <p> Regionally , any future attempts to rearm Hezbollah , Hamas , or PIJ will trigger a ferocious Israeli response . The illusion that Iran can act with impunity has been shattered , to the quiet satisfaction of much of the Middle East , including Saudi Arabia , the United Arab Emirates , and even Iran 's nominal ally , Qatar . <p> --&gt; <p> The loop of perpetual Middle East conflict has , for now , been broken . Iran 's terror proxies are isolated , vulnerable , and on the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the strategic initiative lies firmly with Israel . <p> Militarily , the Jewish state has never been stronger , but it is being comprehensively bested on the battlefield of international public opinion over the conflict in Gaza . With Iran momentarily cowed and Syria making kissy noises , this is Israel 's best window yet to resolve the Palestinian question . This is the chance to trade restraint for recognition and to convert tactical dominance into a durable regional peace through revitalised and expanded Abraham Accords . <p> South Africa faces its own fork in the road . It can continue acting as a megaphone for radical Islam and a cheerleader for rogue states or it , too , can seize the moment . Re-establishing ties with Israel , mending its fractured relationship with Washington , and offering support in the quest for lasting peace in the Middle East would not only serve our national interest , it would rescue a South African foreign policy that under Ramaphosa 's timorous captaincy is adrift in an ANC sea of moral incoherence. 
@@126389900 <p> 1 . I am an advocate of the High Court of South Africa and a member of the Cape Bar . I hold chambers on the 6th floor of Keerom Street Chambers , 56 Keerom Street , Cape Town . I am an adjunct senior lecturer in the faculty of law at the University of the Western Cape . <p> 2 . I am a member of UCT 's Council , and the twenty - sixth respondent in this application . I am cited in this application in my capacity as a member of the Council . I was appointed by the City of Cape Town 's Council to serve on the UCT Council in February 2024 . I have been a member of the Council since 9 February 2024 . <p> 3 . I abide the decision of this Court . <p> 4 . I am constrained to depose to this affidavit in order to fulfil my obligations of candour to the Court . As a member of the Council , I exercise public power . The Constitutional Court has held that this means that I have heightened obligations in litigation . Certain of the facts deposed to on behalf of UCT 's Council are incorrect and my duty is to provide the Court with the correct facts . <p> --&gt; <p> 5 . The Council is a deliberative body that consists of 30 members . These members have different perspectives . The Council has split narrowly on whether the impugned resolutions should continue to exist as a policy of UCT . There is also dissensus within the Council about the resolutions . The Supreme Court of Appeal has recognised that the appropriate course of action for a deliberative body in these instances , is for it to abide the decision of the Court . When this occurs , it is appropriate that the different perspectives be placed before the Court . <p> 6 . I was present at the meeting of the Council on 22 June 2024 , where the impugned resolutions were taken . <p> 7 . The facts set out in this affidavit fall within my personal knowledge , unless the context indicates otherwise . I believe these facts to be true and correct . Where information does not fall within my personal knowledge , but arises from documents that I have considered , I refer to those documents in this affidavit . <p> 8 . Below , I address the following topics : <p> 8.1 . In PART I , I explain the purpose of deposing to this affidavit . <p> 8.2 . In PART 11 , I deal with the meeting of the Council on 22 June 2024 where the impugned resolutions were taken . <p> 8.3 . In PART Ill , I deal with the Council 's meeting on 27 July 2024 , where a motion was tabled to delay the implementation of the impugned resolutions pending a due diligence investigation into the consequences arising from the impugned resolutions . <p> 8.4 . In Part IV , I deal with the Council meetings on 5 and 19 October 2024 where the Council considered this application and took certain decisions relating to the application . <p> 8.5 . In PART V , I deal with the meeting of March 2025 , where a motion was tabled to rescind the impugned resolutions . <p> 8.6 . In PART VI , I deal with the attempts by members of the Council to obtain information and clarity concerning certain facts that were not disclosed at the meeting of 22 June 2024 , and the consistency of certain statements made on affidavit , with statements made in the Council . <p> I. THE PURPOSE OF THIS AFFIDAVIT <p> 9 . On 9 May 2025 , the Chairperson of UCT 's Council filed an affidavit on behalf of the Council in support of Council 's opposition to this application . I received the affidavit on that date through my attorney , and the affidavit was made available to all members of Council on 11 May 2025 . <p> 10 . This is the first time that members of Council had sight of the affidavit filed by the Chair on behalf of the Council . As a member of Council , I was not invited to comment on the affidavit or confirm its contents before it was filed . <p> 11 . A draft of the affidavit was not made available to members of Council for their comment before it was filed . <p> 12 . The Chair 's answering affidavit was made available on a platform called ' Convene ' . This is the application used by members of Council to view documents in a secure manner . Convene does not allow members of Council to download or print documents uploaded to it . When I joined the Council , I was informed that the purpose of the Convene application is to prevent leaks of confidential information by members of Council to those outside of the Council . <p> 13 . Accordingly , I am not able to annex some of the documents that I reference in this application . Certain documents referred to , have also been marked confidential , but I believe that it is necessary to deal with these documents in order to fulfil my obligations to this Court . UCT 's Code of Conduct permits the disclosure of information confidential to the Council where it is legally mandated . <p> 14 . The answering affidavit of the Chair of Council contains information that I was not previously aware of . <p> 15 . It also contains documents that I had not seen before receiving the answering <p> 16 . And it contains certain statements that are not consistent with the facts . <p> 17 . The following are examples of information that I was unaware of and documents that I have seen for the first time because of the litigation : <p> 17.1 . The first time that I became aware of the reason for the non - disclosure of the Donald Gordon Foundation ( DGF ) Donor Agreement and the Dell Foundation agreement , was from the answering affidavit of the Chair and the confirmatory affidavits filed by Exco members , who were present at the meeting of 22 June 2024 . To the best of my knowledge , these members have not disclosed to the Council that the reason for their non <p> - disclosure was because they regarded the information as premature and unripe for Council 's consideration . <p> 17.2 . There were two main reasons suggested for non - disclosure in the Council . The first is that disclosure would not have made a difference to the outcome of the Council 's decision on 22 June 2024 , and the second is that the information was so widespread that it did not require disclosure . <p> 17.3 . I had sight of the letter of the DGF dated 30 April 2024 placing UCT in breach of the donor agreement for the first time when I saw it annexed to the answering affidavit . The letter was not disclosed to members of the Gouncil . <p> 17.4 . The same is true for the DGF 's decision to cancel the donor agreement . <p> Although I suspected that the donor agreement may have been cancelled after reading the supplementary founding affidavit , this fact was never in terms disclosed to the Council . It is certainly the case that the cancellation letter was never placed before the Council . I saw the cancellation letter for the first time when the DGF wrote to UCT Council members on 22 May 2025 , which letter included as an annexure , the cancellation letter dated 6 August 2024 . <p> 18 . I also regard the following statements in the answering affidavit as not being candid with this Court : <p> 18.1 . Paragraph 294 of the answering affidavit is inconsistent with what actually occurred at the Council meeting on 15 March 2025 . The Chair states that " the VC 's verbal report to Council members at the meeting on 15 March 2025 and his exchanges with Council members aligned with his written report " . That is not correct : although the VC 's report suggested a partial recission of the impugned resolutions ; the VC did in fact ask the Council at this meeting to rescind the resolutions altogether . It is incorrect to state that his remarks in the Council " aligned " with his report . <p> 18.2 . I also do not regard paragraph 310.4 of the answering affidavit as correct . In explaining why certain documents did not serve before Council when it took the decision on 22 June 2024 , the Chair states that any member of Council could have sought documents from the Registrar regarding previous Council meetings . This is not accurate . I set out below my email correspondence with the Registrar and the Chair of Council which demonstrates this to be incorrect . <p> 18.3 . Paragraph 577 is also not entirely candid . The Chair states that the terms of reference of the ad hoe committee established to investigate the reasons for non - disclosure " were drafted by Professor Moultrie " . It is correct that Prof Moultrie provided an initial draft of the terms of reference , but this was later supplemented by members of Council . I repeatedly asked the Chair if implicated members of Council added to the terms of reference , because they would be conflicted from doing so . The Chair refused to answer me . I set out this correspondence below . <p> 18.4 . The supplemented terms of reference were of considerable concern to me because they included items that were retaliatory towards Prof Moultrie . The retaliation was based on him merely raising a legitimate governance concern : the non - disclosure issue at the Council meeting of 22 June 2024 . One of the supplemented items on the terms of reference was for an " appropriate sanction " to be brought against Prof Moultrie if it is found that his allegations were without merit . <p> 18.5 . Paragraph 545 of the answering affidavit is not accurate . The Chair states that the record was filed on 13 September 2024 . The same allegation is made in UCT 's answering affidavit in the chamber book application brought by the applicant , which sought to compel the filing of an answering affidavit by the Council , when it had failed to do so timeously . <p> 18.6 . It is correct to state that the Council resolved to ratify the Chair 's decision to file the rule 53 record on 13 September 2024 . That ratification decision was taken on 19 October 2024 . And after the ratification decision was taken , members of Council were invited to supplement the record . <p> 18.7 . The transcript of the impugned meeting of 22 June 2024 was , as a fact , only filed on 12 December 2024 . It was not filed on 13 September 2024 . At best , there was a partial record filed on 13 September 2024 . The Chair recognises at paragraph 673.11 that a redacted transcript for the meeting on 22 June 2024 was only delivered on 12 December 2024 . It is difficult to understand why the Chair denies a delay in filing the record at para 545 of the answering affidavit , when he accepts at para 673.11 that the redacted transcript was only filed on 12 December 2024 . <p> 18.8 . There were good reasons for the delay in filing a complete rule 53 record within the 15-day period prescribed by Uniform Rule 53(1) ( b ) . As an organ of state with heightened constitutional obligations , it is not appropriate for UCT to plead facts which are not entirely accurate . UCT should accept that an incomplete record was filed on 13 September 2024 and explain that there were good reasons for this . <p> 19 . After considering the answering affidavit , and on 20 May 2025 , I requested the Interim Registrar of UCT to provide me with the recordings of certain Council meetings . I annex this request as KP1 . I did so because I wanted to establish whether my recollection of certain Council meetings was correct ; and also , to establish whether other statements made in the answering affidavit concerning Exco meetings , were correct . <p> 20 . On 23 May 2025 , I received a letter from UCT 's attorneys in this matter informing me that - <p> " You are required to comply with the Uniform Rules of Court to obtain documents and recordings for the purpose of the litigation , specifically rule 35 . This must be done via the attorneys of record for you . <p> Should you wish to enter the fray by requesting documents or recordings and filing affidavits in the proceedings , you should withdraw your notice to abide , and if you support the relief Prof Mendelsohn is seeking , should join as co - applicants . <p> Please note that all queries relating to the litigation must be addressed to our offices and not to the Registrar Interim directly " . <p> 21 . This letter is annexed as KP2 . <p> 22 . I was surprised by the letter because I sought the documents from the Registrar , as a member of Council , in order to establish whether certain facts pleaded by the Chair accorded with my recollection of events in Council meetings ; and also , whether certain averments pleaded were correct . <p> 23 . The letter suggested that my filing of any affidavit would be improper , and that I was required to join as a co - applicant . I am aware that a respondent can not support the relief sought by an applicant without joining as a co - applicant . 1 I abide the decision of this Court . I am constrained , as part of an organ of state to place facts before this Court that are relevant and material to the Court exercising its constitutionally entrenched review function . <p> 24 . As a member of the Council , I have heightened constitutional obligations in litigation . The Constitutional Court has held that this means that I may not " mislead or obfuscate . I must do right , and I must do it properly . I am required to be candid and place a full and fair account of the facts before a court " .2 Placing the full and correct facts before a Court exercising its review function does not mean that a respondent supports the relief of the applicant . It means that they have sought to fulfil the obligation to assist the Court with the facts necessary for the Court 's consideration of the matter . <p> 25 . The Chair of Council has repeatedly suggested that it would be improper for me to file this affidavit . He has done so in a threatening manner . At the meeting of 19 October 2024 , the Chair indicated that Council takes collective decisions , and that anyone who files an affidavit will have their tenure on Council called into question . At a recent special meeting on 12 June 2025 , the Chair similarly suggested that it would be improper for me to file an affidavit because of the Council decision to oppose the application . <p> 26 . Other Council members have indicated to me that they would like to share their perspectives with the Court on matters addressed in the answering affidavit , but that they are fearful of doing so . They are concerned about their tenure being called into question , retaliatory conduct by other members of Council , and being labelled as " pro Zionist ! anti- Gaza " or ' Zionists with a political agenda ' on Council . These are phrases used by some members of Council regarding other members of Council who have called a special meeting to deal with their concerns about the governance of UCT . <p> 27 . In my view , it is the culture of secrecy together with the culture of fear , that has resulted in the leaks of confidential documentation to the applicant that the Chair has complained about in his affidavit . The leaks are deeply regrettable . I confirm under oath that I have not leaked any confidential material to the applicant or his legal representatives . My experience on the Council is that the emphasis on confidentiality is rooted in an intention to conceal information to avoid accountability , 3 rather than to act openly and transparently , as is required of every organ of state . I set out examples of this below . <p> 28 . As a deliberative body , it is to be expected that there will be different views and perspectives . <p> 29 . There should be no impediment to members of Council placing their perspectives before the Court . <p> 30 . This is particularly the case where the outcome of the impugned decision is decided by a narrow majority and there is serious internal dissensus on the issue . <p> 31 . At the meeting of 15 March 2025 , the Council considered a motion to rescind the impugned resolutions and withdraw its opposition in this matter . <p> 32 . The Chair addresses this in the answering affidavit at paras 11.2.2 , 12 and 298 . <p> He does not disclose the outcome of the vote . <p> 33. 13 members supported rescinding the impugned resolutions and withdrawing the opposition to this application.14 members voted against the motion . There was 1 abstention . The vote could not be narrower . <p> 34 . The SCA has recognised that where deliberative bodies are narrowly split on a decision , and that decision is later challenged , the appropriate course of action is to abide the decision of the Court rather than oppose it.4 The same is true where there is serious internal dissensus amongst members of the body . The different perspectives should be placed before a Court so that it may exercise its constitutionally entrenched review function . <p> 36 . Accordingly , the suggestion that filing this affidavit is inappropriate is inconsistent with basic constitutional norms that regulate the duties of organs of state that exercise public power . <p> 37 . I , like all members of Council , have a duty of candour to the Court , which means that if I consider certain information to be incorrect , or that the information before the Court is incomplete , I have duty to place the correct and full facts before the Court . <p> 38 . Organs of state bear this duty regardless of whether those facts are to their benefit or detrimental to the case . <p> 39 . Being candid does not mean that a respondent supports the relief of the applicant - it means that they take seriously their duty to the Courts . <p> 40 . Before turning to the meeting of 22 June 2024 , there are two considerations that I emphasise . <p> 41 . The first is that I recognise that all facts after 22 June 2024 are irrelevant to the review . The Court is required to consider whether the Council acted constitutionally and lawfully on 22 June 2024 . <p> 42 . But the founding ( including the supplement thereto ) and the answering affidavits have dealt with facts which occurred after the impugned decision was taken . There is an important reason for this . <p> 43 . The facts that followed the meeting of 22 June 2024 cast a shadow back to this meeting . They concern , inter alia , attempts by members of Council to understand why certain facts were not disclosed to it when the impugned resolutions were voted on . They also raise important matters for the governance of UCT , and the rationality of proceeding with a decision where the information before the Council is incomplete . <p> 44 . The second consideration is the deeply contested nature of this application , and the broad public interest that it has attracted . The impugned resolutions have evoked support and condemnation from various sections of UCT and society generally . <p> 45 . Much has been said in the media about the resolutions , and various interest groups have also sought to write to the Council expressing their views about the resolutions . <p> 46 . The answering affidavit is correct when it states the context of the war in Gaza and Israel 's conduct can not be ignored . Council members who supported the resolutions at the June meeting may well have done so out of a sense of moral conviction . I too have expressed my personal view in the Council , on multiple occasions , concerning Israel 's conduct in Gaza , and the grave humanitarian crisis arising from it . Another member of Council who voted against the resolutions has publicly made similar statements . <p> 47 . It is possible to hold views critical of Israel 's conduct , and at the same time , consider that the deliberations of the Council on 22 June 2024 raise serious governance concerns for UCT . These two ideas are not mutually exclusive . <p> 48 . I believe that an important perspective is lost if the debate is regarded as binary . <p> 49 .
