The correspondence also indicates that the allegations by the DGF and Dell are " hearsay " . The purpose of the meeting was to explain why the facts are incorrect . <p> 230 . I annex these emails as KP12 . <p> 231 . On 18 June 2025 , the Council received legal advice indicating that the special meeting could not go ahead and would be unlawful if ii did . The Council also had different advice indicating that the meeting could go ahead , provided there was a fair opportunity for affected members to address the allegations . <p> 232 . Acting out of an abundance of caution , those who called the special meeting decided that the meeting should be cancelled . We did not want to act contrary to legal advice , even though there was other advice indicating that the meeting could go ahead , provided that there was a fair opportunity to respond to the allegations . I annex the email cancelling the call for a special meeting as KP13 . <p> 233 . All of these facts show that there has been an attempt in the Council to hold its members accountable for the non - disclosure that took place on 22 June 2024 , but the attempts have not been successful . I have raised a Code of Conduct complaint concerning the non - disclosure on 25 March 2025 , and at the time of deposing to this affidavit , the Council is yet to decide what should happen with my complaint . <p> 234 . The Council has also sought to raise questions about the ripeness of the non - disclosure in the light of certain remarks made in the Council , in particular the suggestion that the information was so widespread and that it was assumed that other members of Council knew about the information . I have also asked certain Council members whether they agreed before the meeting of 22 June 2024 , not to disclose the DGF agreement through my written questions . These questions were not responded to . <p> 235 . It is also necessary for the Council to consider why the Chair signed the DGF agreement , in the light of his comments that the agreements violate academic freedom and free speech . This contention is made after UCT breached the agreement , which notice was not brought to the Council 's attention . There are legitimate concerns about why a breach letter was not ripe for disclosure to Council , considering the implications arising from this for Council members ' ability to govern . <p> 236 . UCT is not a hapless contracting party who is at the whim of other contracting parties . It is a sophisticated organ of state with ample legal resources available to it . Legitimate concerns about free speech and limits to it created by donor agreements should be debated and considered by the Council , and the Council should resolve how this is to be addressed with donors . It may be the case that the parties are able to agree to a suitable amendment that addresses all concerns . <p> 237 . But in order to reach decisions about the best way forward , the Council must have all of the necessary information to make these decisions . <p> 238 . Since adopting the impugned resolutions , the Council has constantly received new information concerning the consequences of passing the impugned resolution . <p> 238.1 . The first piece of information was the fact of a donor agreement , which was discovered from the founding affidavit . The founding affidavit did not disclose who the donor was . I only inferred that the donor was the DGF when I was directed to the May 2024 Exco minutes . <p> 238.2 . Ahead of the Council meeting on 7 December 2024 , it was discovered that the Neuroscience Institute was not consulted at all about the passing of the impugned resolutions . The Neuroscience Institute Governing Board wrote to the Council on 20 November 2024 , expressing concerns about the passing of the resolutions without consulting them . The Council made a decision directly affecting the work of the Institute without hearing from them first . <p> 238.3 . The possibility of a donation for a private academic hospital was only discovered after reading the minutes for the meeting of 19 October 2024 . I was unaware on 22 June 2024 , that the decision could affect the development of a hospital which has the potential to benefit a large number of people both at UCT and the broader Cape Town community . There was no opportunity to deliberate on this potential consequence for UCT and the broader community . <p> 238.4 . The Council did not know that the Dell Foundation had suspended funding when it took its decision on 22 June 2024 . <p> 238.5 . The Council also did not have a full appreciation for the financial consequences of its decision for the University . At each Council meeting , new facts concerning these consequences are brought to the Council 's attention . <p> 238.6 . And the Council was not aware of the engagements between the DGF and the University as well as Dell and the University . These facts , from the perspective of both donors , have been brought to the Council 's attention . The Council has not been able to obtain the perspective of the Exco members . <p> 239 . It is likely that the consequences of Council 's decision will not be fully known any time soon . And it is also clear from the above that the factual issues engaged in this application have given rise to strong dissensus within the Council . <p> KESSLER PERUMALSAMY <p> Deposed on 25 June 2025 <p> Footnotes : <p> 1 African Transformation Movement v Speaker of the National Assembly 2023 3 All SA 58 ( WCC ) paras 76- 77 . @@126451511 <h> OPINION <h> IAMHIST 's discriminatory gatekeeping at UCT <p> Sworn statements demanded of Israeli participants disowning any ties to the IDF <p> Divestment over diversity - IAMHIST faces backlash over political gatekeeping <p> The upcoming IAMHIST 2025 Conference , scheduled to take place at the University of Cape Town ( UCT ) on 30 June , is facing escalating scrutiny amid revelations of discriminatory conditions placed on Israeli scholars . What was intended as a premier international gathering of academics in film , media studies , and history is now attracting global criticism for undermining its own founding principles of academic freedom and inclusion . <p> At the centre of the controversy is the treatment of Israeli academics - some of whom have long-standing professional ties to IAMHIST ( the International Association for Media and History ) - whose eligibility to attend the conference has been called into question not on the basis of scholarship or conduct , but solely on nationality . <p> IAMHIST 's own statement on diversity affirms an unambiguous commitment to openness : " The Council of the International Association of Media and History pledges @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ individuals who share our field 's commitments to diverse and multiple national origins , races , ethnicities , religions , genders , sexual orientations , classes , abilities , migration statuses , and other expressions or categorisations of identity ... All IAMHIST activities will be programmed and coordinated with this vision in mind . " Yet that commitment appears to have been fundamentally compromised . <p> Emails which I have personally seen reveal that Associate Professor Martha Evans , UCT academic and local conference organiser , requested sworn statements from Israeli applicants confirming they have no ties ( direct or indirect ) to the Israel Defence Forces ( IDF ) or the broader Israeli military establishment . This demand , made outside any formal university disciplinary process , was not extended to participants of other nationalities , nor rooted in any binding IAMHIST policy . <p> The justification for this unprecedented move lies in UCT Council 's controversial " Gaza Resolution , " passed in April 2024 and still under judicial review . That resolution prohibits UCT academics from engaging with any research group affiliated with the IDF . However @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2025 that in their interpretation this policy applies strictly to individuals currently affiliated with the IDF - not to Israeli nationals or academics in general . <p> --&gt; <p> Despite this , Israeli applicants - who hold academic posts with no military links - were singled out and asked to provide affidavits . The discriminatory nature of this vetting process , especially in a conference hosted under the banner of diversity , has provoked outrage among scholars , legal analysts , and civil rights organisations . <p> While IAMHIST has not issued a public statement defending the excluded scholars , it is understood that senior figures within the organisation have acknowledged serious discomfort with UCT 's demands . The decision to accept the requirement for sworn statements appears to have been made reluctantly , reportedly to protect UCT organisers from potential institutional consequences . <p> IAMHIST leadership has also recognised that the presence of Israeli participants might draw objections from certain quarters , raising broader concerns around reputational risk and the safety of delegates . Assurances have been made that precautions would be taken should such participants attend , implicitly @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ despite the conference 's stated commitment to inclusivity . <p> Concerns over the discriminatory vetting process were formally raised with IAMHIST 's Diversity Officer , Dr Ciara Chambers , IAMHIST President Leen Engelen , Vice President Tobias Hochscherf , and local organiser Martha Evans . The response from IAMHIST leadership was unequivocal : they would not be engaging with the issue . <p> --&gt; <p> That refusal to act - or even publicly acknowledge the implications - has prompted accusations that IAMHIST is turning a blind eye to a blatant breach of its diversity commitments . <p> Further deepening the controversy is the unexamined political affiliation of other participants . One of the conference 's featured speakers , Jihan El Tahri , an acclaimed Egyptian-French filmmaker and a figure affiliated with the Palestine Film Institute , has not been asked to disavow any alignment with Hamas or other proscribed organisations , despite the fact that such associations would trigger scrutiny in many of the home countries represented at the conference . <p> To be clear , El Tahri 's credentials are not in question . But the glaring disparity , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ while others are given a free pass , raises serious questions about the integrity and impartiality of the conference 's organisers . <p> Adding to the sense of hypocrisy is UCT 's broader academic posture . The same university that demands ideological disclaimers from Israelis for attending a local conference has allowed its own academics to engage in relations with Israeli scholars abroad without hesitation or penalty . This selective rigidity reveals a politically motivated double standard , not a principled stand on academic ethics . <p> --&gt; <p> IAMHIST 's handling of this situation , particularly its refusal to formally address or correct the discriminatory measures , has not gone unnoticed . Several academic and legal advocacy groups have now called on the association to reaffirm its commitment to inclusion by applying its principles evenly and without fear or favour . <p> The 2025 IAMHIST Conference now risks being remembered not for its intellectual contributions , but for moral failure . In capitulating to politicised pressure and permitting the exclusion of Israeli voices under the guise of procedural necessity , IAMHIST has not only betrayed its own diversity charter @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ globally . <p> If IAMHIST 's leadership is unwilling to stand by its own values in moments of challenge , it must be asked : what does its commitment to inclusion truly mean ?

Iran 's new precariousness as a functional state , its wavering status as a revolutionary beacon and its more circumscribed financial circumstances reduce Monday 10 30 June 2025 its usefulness to the ANC on all fronts . <p> Whether its nuclear programme has been destroyed or only set back months , Iran undoubtedly will have reduced capacity for mischief-making @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Infrastructure has to be rebuilt , lost nuclear and military expertise replaced and weaponry stocks replenished , all while fretting whether the ayatollah 's government might be toppled in a popular uprising . <p> Iran 's vulnerabilities have been exposed and its confidence has been shaken . <p> Israel effortlessly thwarted its much-hyped missile barrages , while the US demonstrated decisively that it would not sit idle while Tehran lashed out . <p> The loop of perpetual Middle East conflict has been broken , or at least dramatically reset . Iran 's terror proxies are isolated , vulnerable and on the back foot . <h> Israel 's upper hand and mending ties <p> For the first time in decades , the strategic initiative lies firmly with Israel . <p> Militarily , the Jewish state has never been stronger but it is being comprehensively bested on the battlefield of international public opinion over the conflict in Gaza . <p> With Iran down , for the moment , and Syria making noises , this is Israel 's best window yet to resolve the Palestinian question ; to trade restraint for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ peace through a revitalised and expanded Abraham Accords . <p> South Africa faces its own fork in the road . It can continue acting as a megaphone for radical Islam and a cheerleader for rogue states or it , too , can seize the moment . <p> Re-establishing ties with Israel , mending its fractured relationship with Washington and offering itself as a credible , post-ideological broker in the Middle East would not only serve the national interest , it would rescue a South African foreign policy now adrift in ANC moral incoherence , under Ramaphosa 's timorous captaincy .
